MSCI 降評風險升高，印尼股市保守看待
2026 年 10 月 27 日
一、 市場動態
1. MSCI 擬將印尼降至獨立市場，降評風險明顯升高：MSCI 於 10 月 15 日宣布，啟動
將印尼股市由「新興市場（Emerging Market）」 直接降至「獨立市場（Standalone
Market）」的諮詢程序，意見徵詢將於 2027 年 4 月 30 日截止，最晚於 2027 年 6 月
30 日公布最終決定。MSCI 原先於今年 1 月提出可能降至「邊境市場（Frontier Market
）」，此次則跳過「邊境市場」提出直接將該國股市降至「獨立市場」的方案，顯示對

印尼市場治理的疑慮進一步加深。雖然「邊境市場」仍可能是替代分類方案，但 MSCI
強調，除非有明確證據顯示市場治理及運作出現實質改善，否則預期將依目前提案重
新分類，顯示此次降評風險較過去顯著升高。
2. 股權透明度與交易秩序問題為 MSCI 主要疑慮：MSCI 指出，印尼股市長期存在股權
結構不透明、實際自由流通股比例難以確認、疑似協調交易，以及市場監理執法成效
不足等問題，影響國際投資人對市場公平性與可投資性的信心。雖然印尼主管機關已
提出強化持股超過 1%股東資訊揭露、建立高持股集中度（HSC）監理機制，以及規
劃逐步將最低自由流通股比例提高至 15%等改革措施，但 MSCI 認為相關改革尚未展
現持續且具實質性的改善成果。由於問題涉及市場制度與治理結構，改善所需時間可
能較一般短期市場波動更長。
3. 股市反彈後再度回落，外資撤離壓力仍高：受降評消息衝擊，印尼雅加達綜合指數 10
月 16 日重挫 11.57%，創 1998 年以來最大單日跌幅，19 日再跌 3.64%，盤中最低

觸及 4,907.37 點。雖然 20 日至 23 日連續四個交易日反彈，但 26 日再度下跌 1.22%
，收於 5,373.09 點，各產業類股全面收黑，顯示市場信心仍不穩定。根據 Reuters
報導，截至 10 月 19 日，2026 年外資已累計淨賣超印尼股票約 48.8 億美元。此外，
印尼證交所 26 日宣布，將引進獨立專家及外國投資人代表參與高持股集中度審查，
並自 11 月起加強調查疑似協調交易，必要時凍結可疑帳戶，以回應 MSCI 對市場制
度的疑慮。
二、 下跌原因分析：
1. 股市：潛在指數剔除與資金外流，持續壓抑市場評價
若印尼股市最終降至「獨立市場」，原則上將不再納入 MSCI 新興市場及邊境市場相關
指數，追蹤相關指數的被動式基金將須依規則調整持股，實際處理方式仍以 MSCI 後
續公告為準。若改降至「邊境市場」，則可能獲得部分邊境市場資金承接，衝擊相對較
小。Reuters 引述多家金融機構估計，印尼股市降至獨立市場可能引發約 30 億至 60

億美元被動資金流出，其中 UBS 估計約 40 億美元，並指出主動型基金另有約 150 億
美元潛在撤資風險，惟實際規模仍視最終分類結果及基金策略而定。
2. 匯率：外資撤離增加印尼盾貶值壓力
印尼股市的降評風險不僅衝擊股市，也可能透過外資撤離增加印尼盾貶值壓力。印尼
盾 10 月 16 日盤中一度貶至 18,070 兌 1 美元，為 7 月下旬以來最弱水準之一，當日
收盤約 18,000。若後續國際資金持續流出，可能進一步增加股匯市波動，對以外幣計
價、且未完全避險的印尼基金及 ETF 形成額外壓力。雖然印尼央行可透過匯市干預及

流動性管理措施穩定金融市場，但在資金外流壓力尚未緩解前，印尼盾仍可能維持偏
弱格局。
3. 基金與 ETF：指數權重下滑，單一市場曝險風險升高
MSCI 相關指數調整已對印尼股票產生實質影響。Reuters 引用 LSEG 資料顯示，印尼
在 MSCI 新興市場指數的權重已由 2026 年初的 1.16%降至 9 月底的 0.43%，相關成
分股亦由年初 18 檔減至 9 檔，反映部分指數資金調整壓力已提前出現。對印尼單一
國家基金及 ETF 而言，主要風險來自股市下跌、資金流出及印尼盾波動；主動式基金
雖可透過選股降低部分影響，但仍難以完全迴避整體市場風險。不同產品實際受到的
影響，仍應依追蹤指數、主要持股及匯率避險安排個別評估。
三、 市場看法：
1. 制度性問題難以速解，降評風險將持續牽動市場：我們認為，此次 MSCI 降評風險並
非單純反映經濟成長放緩或企業獲利不佳，而是涉及市場透明度、交易公平性與監理
執法等結構性問題。尤其 MSCI 不僅啟動降評諮詢，更持續限制印尼股票的部分指數

調整，包括不提高外資納入因子、不新增相關成分股，以及不進行向上規模分類調整
，顯示市場可投資性疑慮尚未獲得有效解決。儘管政府試圖推動改革，但制度修正仍
須經過執行與成效驗證，才能重新取得國際投資人的信任，因此短期內降評風險仍難
完全消除。
2. 外資撤離與指數權重下降，市場信心修復恐需較長時間：印尼今年以來外資已大幅賣
超，MSCI 新興市場指數中的權重亦明顯下降，顯示國際投資人對當地市場的疑慮已
逐漸反映於實際資金流向。我們認為，即使印尼最終成功避免降評，部分國際機構投
資人仍可能因市場透明度、流動性及治理品質疑慮而維持較低曝險。此外，MSCI 對
部分指數調整的凍結措施尚未解除，短期內新增指數資金流入機會仍受到限制，預期
國際資金回流與市場評價修復均可能需要較長時間。
3. 監理改革力度加大，但尚不足以扭轉保守展望：印尼政府近期持續加強改革，包括提
高資訊透明度、強化交易監控、對違規交易開罰，以及引進外部獨立人士參與監理審
查。10 月 26 日該國政府進一步提出主動調查疑似協調交易及凍結可疑帳戶等措施，

11 月 2 日起亦規劃將高持股集中度股票排除於雅加達綜合指數之外。相關改革有助改

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
善交易秩序及短期市場信心，但部分措施仍待正式實施，且實際成效尚未獲得 MSCI
認可。考量制度性問題改善需要時間，政策利多雖可能帶動短線反彈，但尚不足以改
變對印尼股市中期前景的保守判斷。
4. 股市跌深不代表利空出盡，中期風險報酬吸引力偏低：印尼股市在 10 月 16 日及 19
日大幅修正後，雖曾連續四個交易日反彈，但 26 日再度回落，顯示市場尚未形成穩
定回升趨勢。我們認為，當前股市面臨的不僅是短期賣壓，更包括國際投資人信任下
降及風險溢價提高，即使企業基本面未同步惡化，仍可能因評價下修而承受股價壓力

。因此，短線不排除政策利多或跌深後出現技術性反彈，但在 MSCI 態度、外資流向
與印尼盾走勢明顯改善前，反彈延續性仍有疑慮，印尼股市中期展望維持保守。
四、市場展望：
1. 印尼股市維持保守展望，單一市場風險報酬吸引力不足：考量 MSCI 降評風險尚未解除、
外資撤離壓力持續，以及市場治理改革仍有待驗證，我們認為印尼股市未來數季的風險報
酬吸引力相對有限。即使近期股價明顯回落，仍難認定相關風險已充分反映。相較市場制
度成熟、國際資金流動性較佳的新興市場，印尼股市可能持續面臨較高風險溢價，因此對
單一印尼市場的集中曝險應維持審慎態度。
2. 此事件對新興市場指數與東協指數影響相對有限：根據 MSCI 資料，截至 2026 年 9 月底
，印尼股市在 MSCI Emerging Market 指數與 MSCI AC ASEAN 指數中的權重分別約為
0.43%與 7.55%，因此我們認為印尼潛在降評對相關指數的直接衝擊相對有限。此外，新
興市場各國經濟結構與產業組成有所差異，區域型基金可透過跨國分散降低單一市場風險

。惟後續仍須留意印尼事件對整體新興市場投資信心造成的外溢影響，以及全球貿易與匯
率波動風險。

AI算力需求推升資料中心擴建，電力與散熱成為建置瓶頸
2030年AI Workload容量上看156GW(CAGR 28.8%)；電網補強需3~5年，機房建置僅需1~2年。
接電時程落差推升現地供電需求，SOFC取得大型AIDC訂單

液冷由晶片延伸至整機與電源，規格與價值量同步升級
Rubin世代全面液冷，CDU放大至2.3~3MW；800VDC電源櫃液冷化，快接頭升級至大口徑與ZQD。

### Page 4

3
AI 算力需求推升科技業者資本支出，帶動資料中心長期擴建
➢ AI算力需求與AIDC建置瓶頸
預估全球AI Workload容量需求將由2025年44GW成長至2030年156GW，CAGR達28.8%，五年新增112GW。顯示AI算力
需求將持續帶動資料中心建置及相關基礎設施投資。
預估北美主要雲端與科技業者(CSP)資本支出將由2025年4,450億美元成長至2028年1.15兆美元，CAGR達37.2%。隨CSP持
續投入算力擴建，將帶動AI伺服器、機櫃、電力及散熱等基礎設施需求，支撐相關供應鏈中長期成長動能。
AI算力需求驅動資料中心擴建，2030年AI Workload容量上看156GW CSP資本支出加速擴張，2028年規模上看1.15兆美元

AIDC 擴建推升用電規模與機櫃密度，供電及散熱能力影響建置進度
➢ AI算力需求與AIDC建置瓶頸
電網擴建與資料中心建置存在時程落差，電力取得速度成為AIDC擴張的重要限制。IEA指出，新建電網從規劃、許可至完工
可能需5–15年，資料中心建置則約1–3年。
AI運算朝高密度系統整合，配電與液冷升級需求涵蓋GPU及CSP自研ASIC。除NVIDIA GB200／GB300外，Google TPU 8t
／8i採第四代液冷，AWS Trainium3 Teton3 MAX採冷板液冷架構，AMD Helios亦已有支援最高246kW雙寬機櫃的合作參
考設計，系統驗證與量產交付能力將成為AIDC建置之關鍵。
全球再生能源與大型儲能併網排隊規模逾2,300 GW 高密度GPU與ASIC機櫃功率上升將持續推動配電及液冷升級

### Page 6

5
電網補強與機房建置存在時程落差，提升AIDC現地供電需求
➢ AI算力需求與AIDC建置瓶頸
AIDC擴張需同步取得發電供應、輸配電容量及場址受電能力，任一環節受限，皆可能影響機房投運時程。隨單一場址用電規
模擴大，部分專案需新增變電站、饋線及相關設備，供電進度因而受到重電設備交期拉長、區域電網併網與饋線容量不足、
熱損耗與轉換效率等因素影響。
NLR指出，美國資料中心建置與負載爬坡通常約需12–24個月，公用事業電網補強則常需3–5年。我們認為，兩者的時程落差
將提高業者評估分階段供電及現地發電的意願，並為燃料電池與燃氣機組等方案創造導入機會；實際效益仍須綜合評估燃料、
許可、可靠度與完整供電成本。
AIDC供電涉及多個環節，場址接電能力影響投運時程 資料中心(AIDC)建置與電網補強時程比較

### Page 7

6
接電時程落差推升現地供電需求，SOFC提供模組化連續供電選項
➢ SOFC現地供電與台廠供應鏈
當電網補強時程無法配合機房投運，業者可評估現地發電，搭配分階段送電與儲能等方式銜接供電需求。方案選擇須綜合考
量連續供電能力、燃料供應、建置條件與可靠度要求。
Bloom Energy採用固態氧化物燃料電池(SOFC)，透過電化學反應將燃料轉換為電力，可依需求配置模組，提供持續性現地
電源。其應用可涵蓋與電網並聯或微電網架構，實際部署仍須配合燃料、許可及場內電力系統設計。
現地發電增加機房供電來源 現地發電與儲能方案比較

註：本圖簡化供電路徑，電力轉換、UPS、儲能與備援配置依案場設計
方案 主要功能 選用時的評估重點
電池儲能 電力銜接 與功率調節 儲能量體、輸出功率及充電來源
柴油發電機 停電備援 依額定選擇運轉用途 燃料補給、設備額定及適用許可
燃氣渦輪 可配置為 持續發電來源 天然氣供應與工程、運轉條件

SOFC燃料電池 模組化配置 持續發電 燃料供應、高溫啟停及電堆維護
註：實際用途依額定與案場設計；天然氣SOFC仍有CO2排放

### Page 8

7
SOFC以電化學反應直接發電，模組化整合支援資料中心現地供電
➢ SOFC現地供電與台廠供應鏈
BE採固態氧化物燃料電池(SOFC)，氧離子穿過固態電解質與燃料進行電化學反應直接發電，電子經外部迴路流動，形成直
流電，不經燃燒，電效率達 53–65%。SOFC由多片電池組成電堆，再整合為發電模組，搭配燃料處理、空氣供應、熱管理
與電力轉換設備，形成完整供電系統。
一座1GW的資料中心採用濕式冷卻燃氣渦輪機聯合循環發電系統，在其使用壽命期間，大約會消耗250億加侖的水；但若是
用燃料電池僅需140萬加侖。
氧離子穿越陶瓷電解質，電子經外部迴路形成電力 電池片逐級整合為電堆、發電模組與供電系統
資料來源：IEA、兆豐投顧整理 資料來源：AEP、兆豐投顧整理

### Page 9

8
發電效率與部署條件，決定SOFC在AIDC的採用機會
➢ SOFC現地供電與台廠供應鏈
AIDC持續運轉將累積大量燃料支出，提高發電效率可降低相同電力輸出所需的天然氣量。SOFC具高效率發電特性，其經濟
價值須結合燃料價格、使用時數及維護成本評估。
SOFC、燃氣引擎與燃氣渦輪各有部署及運轉條件。業主須共同評估設備交付、場址工程、燃氣供應、排放許可及維運需求，
才能判斷適合的現地供電方案。
資料來源：兆豐投顧試算 資料來源：DOE、GE、Caterpillar、BE、兆豐投顧整理

政策支援燃料電池投資，木星計畫燃氣與許可牽動落地時程
➢ SOFC現地供電與台廠供應鏈
木星計畫位於美國新墨西哥州，為Oracle支援OpenAI Stargate建置的AI資料中心園區。Oracle於2026年4月宣布，規劃採

木星計畫的SOFC方案仍以天然氣供應為前提，燃氣接入與空氣品質許可均影響建置進度。州土地辦公室7月維持拒發特定管

SOFC擴建帶動高溫熱管理與電力線纜需求，台廠切入不同供應環節
➢ SOFC現地供電與台廠供應鏈
依Ceres基於BNEF資料之分析，2030年全球SOFC市場機會約22GW，其中資料中心應用占49%(約10.8GW)，為最大單一
應用市場。隨AI資料中心擴建與供電需求提升，資料中心可望成為SOFC商業化的重要需求來源，帶動高溫熱管理與電力零
組件的應用機會。
SOFC系統需要高溫熱交換、電力轉換與系統配線等零組件。高力提供熱反應器、複熱器等高溫熱管理關鍵零組件；宏致則
供應高溫反應模組耐高溫電纜、電力控制與轉換模組內電纜，以及系統管理與電力傳輸線纜。

### Page 13

12
供電與液冷協同升級，支撐AIDC算力密度與能源效率提升
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
在總供電及機房空間受限下，AIDC須兼顧能源與空間使用效率。降低冷卻與配電等基礎設施耗能，可在相同總用電條件下提
高IT設備用電占比；評估整體效益時，除PUE外，仍須觀察單位運算耗電及單位面積算力。
液冷透過冷板、分歧管與CDU將熱帶至設施端，高壓直流與Power sidecar則調整供電及設備配置。隨散熱需求延伸至電源
模組，供電與冷卻系統須協同設計，兼顧電力轉換、散熱耗能及部署密度。而電源模組採用液冷，全新的水冷資料中心機房
即不再需要傳統的冷熱通道(Hot/Cold Aisle)與大型空調系統，基礎設施CAPEX預估可節省高達10%。
相同總用電下，降低基礎設施耗能可提高IT用電占比 運算與電源同步液冷，降低對機房氣流散熱的依賴
資料來源：DOE、兆豐投顧試算 資料來源：NVIDIA、MEGMEET、兆豐投顧整理
度/電
註：依PUE定義進行情境試算，非特定液冷方案實測結果。IT設備用

電包含伺服器內部風扇等耗電，不等同晶片運算用電。

### Page 14

13
熱捕捉範圍擴大，液冷設計由主晶片延伸至整機架構
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
除CPU、GPU、TPU及ASIC等主要熱源外，液冷進一步涵蓋DIMM、OSFP光模組與板級元件，透過冷板及導熱板提高液體
帶走熱量的比例，降低對氣流散熱的依賴。
為支援不同熱源與機構配置，運算主板與整機伺服器之液冷解決方案多元化，包括冷板迴路式、混合式及整合式大冷板架構。
我們認為，隨液冷涵蓋元件增加，流道與連接設計、模組組裝及可靠度驗證的重要性將提高，供應鏈機會亦由單顆冷板延伸
至整機冷卻整合。
液冷涵蓋主晶片與周邊元件，熱捕捉比例最高達100% 冷板迴路式、混合式與整合式架構支援不同整機配置
資料來源：CoolIT、兆豐投顧整理 資料來源： CoolIT、兆豐投顧整理

### Page 15

14
CDU串接IT與設施冷卻迴路，承接液冷擴張後的排熱需求
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
隨液冷由CPU、GPU延伸至記憶體、光模組及板級元件，液體承擔的設備熱量增加；機櫃功率密度提高，更進一步推升液冷
迴路的排熱需求。CDU串接IT側與設施側，透過熱交換器、循環泵浦與控制系統維持供液條件，其換熱能力與供液穩定性將
成為高密度AIDC的重要設計環節。
CDU升級除提高散熱容量，更須兼顧能源與空間使用效率。OCP 2026的2.3MW系統模擬研究顯示，在相同散熱負載下，比
較方案間全年冷卻系統能耗差距約17%。
冷板取熱、CDU換熱、設施端排熱，構成完整液冷迴路 相同2.3MW散熱負載之下，CDU選型仍牽動流量與全年能耗

註：典型液對液CDU示意圖。紅、藍代表各迴路相對高低溫，非特定
案場溫度。

比較項目 OCP研究結果
散熱負載 各方案均為2.3MW
設施側回水溫度 約34~39°C

差距，並非液冷普遍節電率。2.3MW為散熱負載，非設備耗電量。

Deschutes開放2MW級CDU設計，瞄準能源與空間效率提升
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
Google將第五代CDU設計Deschutes貢獻予OCP，提供高密度AI機房可採用的開放規格。其v1.0設計以2MW散熱容量、
3°C接近溫差及IT側與設施側各500GPM流量為目標，透過三組雙流程熱交換器並聯，兼顧換熱表現與流阻，支撐高熱負載液

浦及溫度、壓力與流量監控，兼顧容量與供液可靠度。我們認為，CDU的競爭重點將涵蓋單位占地容量、低溫差換熱及泵浦
耗能，須從整體冷卻系統評估其能源與空間效益。

800VDC重整供電配置，液冷延伸至高密度電源櫃
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
隨AIDC機櫃功率提高，低電壓供電面臨大電流、導體體積與電源設備占用空間等挑戰。Power sidecar將前端電力轉換與部
分備援設備移至獨立電源櫃，再以800VDC送至運算櫃，並依架構於負載附近降壓；透過提高輸電電壓與調整設備配置，支
援更高功率密度部署。
NVIDIA第二代MGX Power Rack參考設計，朝100%液冷發展，並標示880kW供電能力(N配置、含BBU)及90秒備援供電時
間。隨電力轉換與備援設備集中，液冷需求將進一步延伸至電源模組，使供電與散熱成為能源及空間效率升級的重要環節。
前端電源移至獨立機櫃，800VDC支援高功率密度供電 NVIDIA MGX第二代電源櫃朝全液冷發展，液冷散熱模組與零組件用量提升

液冷導入電源模組，推進功率密度與能源效率升級
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
電源模組導入液冷後，可透過冷板將功率元件的熱量帶至冷卻液，降低對風扇、大型散熱鰭片與風道的需求。相較風冷設計，
液冷有助縮小散熱結構占用空間，並降低風扇耗電及元件工作溫度，使電源模組朝更高功率密度發展。
MEGMEET於2026年10月OCP展示100kW、800V液冷AC/DC電源層，採2RU不含BBU設計，產品標示轉換效率逾98%；另
展示1RU、95kW的800V轉54V液冷DC/DC電源層。我們認為，液冷應用延伸至整流與降壓環節，將擴大電源冷板、流道設
計及模組整合的需求，提升水冷板、快接頭、滑軌、機殼與液冷busbar之需求量。
水冷板承接功率元件熱量，降低電源模組對風扇與風道的依賴 液冷電源層涵蓋前端整流與櫃內降壓，支援緊湊供電配置

晶片TDP與出貨量同步提升，水冷板技術與ASP仍具升級空間
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
晶片TDP逐代提升，NVIDIA由GB300的1.4kW增至Rubin 2.3kW、Rubin Ultra約2.6kW，TPU v9與Trainium4將達3~4kW。
我們認為，至少至Rubin Ultra世代，3kW以內熱負載仍可由目前冷板架構解熱，並透過微通道、雙面水冷板(4→6kW)及鑽
石銅等材料升級持續解熱。
我們預估AI晶片出貨量由2025年1,036萬顆增至2027年2,403萬顆(26/27年YoY+39%/+67%)，其中ASIC於2027年達1,333萬
顆、占比由37%升至55%，超越GPU；出貨放量加上電源櫃與光模組導入液冷，水冷板用量與滲透率仍有提升空間。
TDP持續攀升，水冷板技術與ASP仍有升級空間 AI晶片出貨量預估，整體供應鏈於2027年仍供不應求
資料來源：兆豐投顧預估及整理 資料來源：兆豐投顧預估及整理
註：目前水冷板架構極限約3,000多瓦，現行出貨晶片多未超過3,000瓦，而進入Feynman世代瓦數將達3,500~4,000瓦，須改架構。

Rubin Ultra以雙晶片版2.6kW計；TPU v9、Trainium4取3~4kW中值。

冷板與流體零組件升級，須兼顧散熱效能與系統耗電
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
晶片功耗提升推高冷板流量需求，惟CDU可供IT側使用的壓差有限(Deschutes規格為80~90psi)，冷板與快接頭須以更低壓
降輸送更大流量。Rubin Ultra將導入內建球閥、提升流量的ZQD；我們認為，快接頭技術門檻由防漏延伸至大流量低壓降，
帶動規格與單價升級。
NVIDIA MGX第二代電源櫃全面液冷，以1座電源櫃搭配2座運算櫃為一組試算，每組新增26片電源冷板及52顆快接頭；惟兩
段電源轉換熱損合計僅約供電量4%，增量以件數為主，價值量約為運算櫃快接頭的5%。
電源設備液冷化：每組(1*電源櫃+2*運算櫃)零組件用量試算 流量提升推動快接頭規格升級，Rubin Ultra每tray 10顆MQD改採ZQD
資料來源：NVIDIA、MEGMEET、產業訪談、兆豐投顧整理與預估 資料來源：OCP、NVIDIA、產業訪談、兆豐投顧整理與預估
註：依MGX參考設計880kW、N配置及滿載估算，未含備援、BBU及manifold端對插件；運算櫃快接頭以VR每櫃約450只及manifold 88只計。單價：MQD約US$20、UQD08

約US$30~40；UQD04無實際報價，以小口徑QD均價估計；ZQD最終價格未定。熱負載以轉換效率約98%估算；AC/DC每層以880kW÷8層計。

液冷由晶片延伸至整機與電源，單櫃價值倍增帶動台廠受惠
➢ 液冷系統擴張與AIDC效率升級
液冷由CPU、GPU延伸至記憶體、光模組及電源設備，Rubin世代全面採用100%液冷，CDU容量放大至2.3~3MW。我們預
估，單櫃液冷價值(含CDU)由約10萬美元倍增至約20萬美元，全球液冷市場將由2026年105億美元成長至2030年853億美元
(CAGR約69%)。
價值升級沿三條主線：CDU放大容量與泵浦冗餘、冷板導入雙面水冷與鑽石銅、快接頭朝大口徑低壓降及ZQD升級；電源櫃
液冷則以件數貢獻增量。我們認為，冷板與快接頭兼具規格升級與用量成長，奇鋐、雙鴻、富世達最直接受惠。
全球液冷市場2030年達853億美元，二次側占約7成5 液冷各環節升級方向與單櫃價值量占比

### Page 23

22
結論
➢ 結論
AI算力需求推升資料中心擴建，電力與散熱成為建置瓶頸
2030年AI Workload容量上看156GW(CAGR 28.8%)；電網補強需3~5年，機房建置僅需1~2年。
接電時程落差推升現地供電需求，SOFC取得大型AIDC訂單
Oracle、Nebius、AEP/Google相繼採用BE燃料電池；2030年SOFC市場機會約22GW，資料中心占49%。
液冷由晶片延伸至整機與電源，規格與價值量同步升級
Rubin世代全面液冷，CDU放大至2.3~3MW；800VDC電源櫃液冷化，快接頭升級至大口徑與ZQD。
看好液冷冷板、快接頭與SOFC關鍵零組件供應鏈
奇鋐(3017)、富世達(6805)受惠液冷規格升級；高力(8996)切入SOFC供應鏈，宏致(3605)列入觀察。

### Page 1

2026年10月14日

年6月日

供应链扰动，2026年3月10日

响或有限，2025年10月13日
全球经济温和复苏，价格带动进口增速反超出口
——2026 年 10 月进出口数据及全球经济点评
事件：2026年10月，我国进出口总额为7193.8亿美元，同比
增长 37.9%。其中，出口金额为 4123.3 亿美元，同比增长
35.2%；进口金额为 3070.5 亿美元，同比增长 41.6%；贸易顺
差为 1052.8 亿美元，同比增长 19.6%。
一、10月进出口数据解析
 10 月出口延续高位增长，海外制造业扩张和科技产业链需求

构成主要支撑。10 月出口同比增长 35.2%（以美元计价，下
同），较 9 月小幅放缓 1.1 个百分点，但仍明显高于 2021—
2025年10月约5.9%的平均增速。从外部环境看，10月全球制
造业PMI为52.6%，连续15个月处于扩张区间，全球制造业仍
处上行周期为短期商品贸易提供支撑。值得注意的是，本轮制
造业扩张更多由AI、国防等领域资本开支以及投资品和中间品
需求驱动，相关需求对价格和融资成本的敏感度相对较低，因
而在高利率和通胀压力下仍保持较强韧性。与此同时，能源及
物流扰动导致全球供应商交付周期延长、积压订单增加，部分
经济体面临产能和原材料约束，我国产业链完整、生产响应较
快和交付能力稳定的优势进一步显现，有利于承接全球订单。
此外，去年同期出口基数偏低，也放大了本月同比增幅。
 亚洲区域贸易保持活力，韩国和拉美市场成为本月较为突出
的增量来源。10 月我国对东盟出口同比增长 46.3%，占出口
总额的比重升至 18.9%，对整体出口增速拉动约 8.1 个百分
点，仍是出口增长的最大拉动项。 亚洲产业链内部出现一

定分化，其中对韩国出口增速由 9 月的 68.2%进一步升至
92.4%，拉动出口增长约 3.3 个百分点，是本月主要市场中
最突出的加速项；对中国香港和印度出口分别增长 48.6%和
43.7%，继续保持较快增长。对美欧出口仍具韧性，对美国www.ccxi.com.cn

市场多元化在对冲部分传统市场增速放缓方面发挥了积极作用。

拉动 28.7 个百分点，较9 月提高1.1 个百分点；高新技术产品出口增长 66.6%，拉
动出口增长 18.1 个百分点，较 9 月提高 0.7 个百分点。其中，集成电路出口增长

137.1%，拉动出口增长约 7.5 个百分点，仍是最主要的细分支撑；自动数据处理设
备及其零部件出口增长 114.8%，拉动作用由 9 月的 5.1 个百分点升至 6.1 个百分点，
成为本月科技产品中较为突出的增量来源。相比之下，汽车及底盘出口增速由
62.0%回落至 37.6%，拉动作用由 2.4 个百分点降至 1.8 个百分点；服装、纺织品、
玩具、家用电器、塑料制品和家具出口虽然均保持两位数增长，但合计拉动由 9 月
的约 3.8 个百分点降至2.8 个百分点。总体看，10 月出口高增更多依靠科技产业链
支撑，算力设备的拉动作用进一步增强，而汽车和传统商品贡献边际减弱，出口增
长动力较 9 月有所集中。

 价格因素推动进口增速大幅上升，科技产业链与能源商品共同推动进口增速超过出口。
10月进口同比增长41.6%，较9月加快10.6个百分点，创五年来新高。在出口仍保持
35%以上增长的情况下，进口进一步提速，推动当月贸易顺差由 9 月的 1335.3 亿美元
收窄至1052.8亿美元，减少282.5亿美元。值得注意的是，尽管今年以来进口增速持
续走高，但商品结构集中度也同步上升，且价格对进口总额的带动明显强于规模，能
源品与中间品进口的增多并不代表内需的改善。10 月进口增长动力明显向能源商品扩
展，呈现“科技产业链保持强劲、能源商品拉动跃升”的特征。不过，10 月国内制造
业 PMI 重新回落至收缩区间，铁矿石、农产品等与传统生产和消费需求关联较强的商
品进口仍较弱，内需尚未全面回暖。
 高技术产品仍是进口增长的基础性支撑，能源商品则贡献了本月进口加速的主要
边际增量。10 月高新技术产品进口同比增长 72.1%，对整体进口增速拉动 23.3 个

百分点，较9月提高约2.0个百分点。其中，集成电路进口增长78.3%，拉动进口

进口增长 261.0%，拉动约 8.0 个百分点，虽较 9 月略有减弱，但仍处于较高水平。
更值得关注的是，能源商品对进口增长的合计拉动由 9 月的约 1.3 个百分点大幅

——2026 年 8 月进出口数据点评
议升温，我国科技产品出口对全球科技投资周期的依赖进一步加深，而汽车及传统商品
增长动力有所减弱，出口增长的持续性面临一定考验。贸易政策方面，美国中期选举后
国内政策博弈可能加剧，现有关税安排仍具有阶段性，主要经济体针对重点产业的贸易
限制和供应链本地化趋势亦难以逆转。此外，中东局势反复及能源运输风险仍可能推高
生产和物流成本，进一步压缩企业利润空间。总体看，短期出口仍具较强韧性，但后续
能否保持较快增长，将更多取决于科技投资景气的持续性、传统商品需求恢复及外部贸
易环境的变化。
二、全球经济跟踪：全球经济保持温和增长，通胀黏性与政策不确定性加剧市场调整
近一个月，从宏观经济表现来看，美国经济增长加快，欧洲温和复苏，日本内需仍然偏
弱。通胀黏性与科技投资不确定性成为市场关注重点，一方面，继 9 月美联储加息后，11月
英国央行进一步加息控制通胀水平。另一方面，AI 投资回报担忧引发科技股明显调整，新兴

市场资本外流扩大。全球贸易受AI 产业链需求支撑保持韧性，但能源运输风险和地区分化仍
然突出。此外，11 月 3 日，美国中期选举民主党夺取国会两院控制权，特朗普政府施政面临
更多制约，也给外部环境带来新变局。总体看，全球经济尚具韧性，但增长动力集中、通胀
反弹及政策约束加大，经济金融运行的不确定性进一步上升。
 主要经济体增长分化，能源冲击推动生产端通胀加快上行，货币政策面临更大约
束。美国经济保持较强韧性，三季度 GDP 环比折年实际增长 3.4%，较二季度加快
1.2 个百分点，消费和投资共同支撑增长，其中 AI 相关资本开支持续扩张，但对
就业及居民收入的带动相对有限。9 月 CPI 同比上涨 3.7%，能源价格上涨加大通
胀压力。继 9 月加息 25 个基点后，美联储 10 月维持政策利率在 3.75%—4.00%，
两名委员主张进一步加息，反映通胀风险仍受到高度关注。11 月 3 日中期选举中，
民主党赢得参众两院多数席位，选民对高生活成本和中东战争的不满集中显现。
共和党失去国会控制权后，特朗普政府推动新增财政刺激、税收和其他立法议程

的难度上升，但关税及对外政策仍具有较强行政裁量空间，后续政策博弈可能加
剧经济不确定性。欧洲经济温和修复，日本内需仍显疲弱，通胀压力使得欧、日
央行货币政策维持偏紧。三季度欧元区 GDP 环比增长 0.4%，同比增长1.3%，德国
小幅回升，法国表现偏弱。10 月欧元区通胀升至 4.0%，其中能源价格同比上涨
20.8%，加剧居民购买力和企业成本压力。日本9月核心CPI同比上涨2.1%，居民

消费恢复缓慢，日本央行 10 月维持政策利率 1.25%不变。总体看，中东能源冲击
使主要央行面临更突出的增长与通胀权衡，货币政策由此前的宽松预期转向维持
限制性立场甚至进一步紧缩，尤其加大了能源进口依赖型经济体的滞胀压力。
 全球金融市场波动加剧，AI 投资回报担忧引发科技股调整，能源价格与利率预期
变化推动资产重新定价。近一个月，金融市场呈现明显的结构性分化。10 月中旬
至今，标普 500 指数下跌约 3.3%，纳斯达克指数下跌约 7.7%，道琼斯指数则上涨
约 1.4%，反映资金由高估值科技股向价值和防御性板块轮动。尽管美国企业盈利
总体保持韧性，但市场对 AI 资本开支持续扩张、债务融资增加及投资回报兑现的
担忧升温，美国中期选举后数据中心建设政策的不确定性进一步加剧市场调整。
债券市场方面，避险需求上升推动美国 10 年期国债收益率由 5.2%左右降至当前
的 4.86%。大宗商品方面，中东局势反复推动油价先涨后跌，布伦特原油 10 月上

涨约13%，11月初随着地缘冲突缓和预期升温明显回落，当前降至约92美元/桶；
黄金亦呈现较大波动。总体看，近期全球资产价格调整是高利率环境下 AI 投资回
报、能源供应风险及政策不确定性共同推动的结构性重新定价。
 全球金融条件偏紧与科技股调整加剧新兴市场资本外流，但我国跨境资金仍保持
净流入。IIF 数据显示，10 月新兴市场证券投资净流出 338 亿美元，较 9 月修订
后的 193 亿美元进一步扩大，为 3 月以来最大单月流出规模，与上年同期净流入
274 亿美元形成鲜明对比。亚洲科技股调整、美元相对强势及主要经济体利率维
持高位，共同加大新兴市场资本流出压力。相比之下，我国跨境资金流动总体稳
健。国家外汇管理局数据显示，9月银行代客涉外收付款顺差407亿美元，连续六
个月保持净流入，但较8月的624亿美元下降约35%；同期银行结售汇顺差562亿
美元，反映境内外汇供求总体平衡、资金流入仍具支撑。结构上，货物贸易项下
资金保持较大规模净流入，但企业对外直接投资及境外关联企业融资有所增加。

总体看，外部金融环境收紧对我国跨境资金流动形成一定扰动，但贸易顺差及相
对稳定的市场预期仍提供支撑，需关注后续资本流入放缓及汇率波动风险。
 中东冲突仍在持续博弈拉锯中，能源运输和全球供应链仍面临较大不确定性。10
月以来，美伊冲突持续围绕霍尔木兹海峡及周边能源设施展开，11 月初多艘商船
遇袭，但卡塔尔等国也重启斡旋，局势出现一定缓和迹象。与此同时，也门政府

Zhen Ding Technology (4958.TW)
10 November 2026 Citi Research
4
Zhen Ding Technology
Valuation
We set a DCF-based TP of NT$750. We use a DCF-based valuation to reflect ZDT's ability to generate stable cash flows. Key
assumptions of our DCF model include a risk-free rate of 5.2% (10-year government bond rate), a market risk premium of 7%,
and an equity beta of 0.89 (1-year weekly share price), deriving a WACC of 10.7%. Our model assumes high-single-digit long-

term growth after 2028E and a 21.6% EBIT margin from 2028E with a gradual annual increase. Our TP is equivalent to 27x/17x
2027E/28E EPS and 4.5x/3.8x 2027E/28E BVPS.
Risks
Key downside risks that could cause the stock to trade below our target price include: 1) lower-than-expected
iPhone/Mac/iPad end-demand; 2) lower content value for new smartphone models or pricing pressure; 3) ZDT losing market
share from its FPC/HDI peers; 4) a slower demand in the substrate industry; 5) failure to pass the qualifications of major AI

GPU or ASIC customers.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

our ratings distribution table for our Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside rating system. However, we reiterate that we do
not consider No View to be a recommendation. For all Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside calls, risk exists that the
catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: We review Hon Hai’s 3Q26 results, announced 12 November. 
We are impressed with its opex control, which led to solid operating margin 
expansion despite gross margin dilution from rising AI server contribution.

What’s the impact: 3Q26 results beat. Net income was TWD78bn (EPS: 
TWD5.53), 25% higher than our estimate (TWD62bn) and 10% higher than 
the Bloomberg consensus (TWD70bn). Revenue was TWD3.0tn (+19% 
QoQ; +46% YoY) with better-than-expected smartphone contribution. The 
gross margin came in at 6.02% (vs. 6.12% in 2Q26), a 0.1pp decline QoQ 
on a rising cloud segment mix. That said, the operating margin expanded 
0.07pp to 3.83%, driven by strong opex control and better leverage.

Key updates: Hon Hai reaffirms its positive view of its 2027 revenue 
momentum, targeting double-digit growth YoY (similar to our forecast). The 
company believes its cloud business will remain a key growth driver (54% 
of total revenue in 3Q26). Regarding total AI server racks, Hon Hai expects 
to see high-double-digit growth in 2027 (vs. our expectation of c.30% YoY 
growth from GPU server plus a rising ASIC contribution; however, as ASIC 
AI servers typically utilise consignment models, its AI server revenue 
growth will be slightly diluted). As for smartphones, considering Hon Hai’s 
key clients’ models command high ASPs, we believe the impact from rising 
memory costs will be relatively smaller, but we expect the PC segment to 
decline YoY due to the negative impact from rising component costs.

During the 3Q26 results call, AI server end demand from the US was the 
hottest question, especially after the US mid-term elections. We believe 
management’s view on hyperscalers’ and neocloud’s near-term demand 
remains positive, but the long-term impact must be closely monitored. Also, 
as investors are worried about the AI server capex slowdown from CSPs, 
management also indicated that there would be new end demand from 
sovereign and enterprise groups, which could act as a second growth 
wave. For 4Q26, the company is guiding for >15% revenue growth QoQ 
and YoY given the ICT peak season along with rising AI server revenue (up 
by 5-15% QoQ due to ongoing AI server shipment strength).

What we recommend: We raise our 2026-28E EPS by 10-12% to reflect 
higher ASP assumptions for AI servers. We reaffirm our Buy (1) rating but 
trim the 12M TP to TWD320, using a lower target PER of 13x (prior 16x; vs. 
its past-3-year range of 8-18x) on our 1-year forward EPS, given the overall 
weaker sentiment. Downside risk: low demand for AI servers.

How we differ: Our 2026-27E revenue are above the consensus by 2-3%, 
likely as we are more optimistic on Hon Hai’s AI server shipments.

12 November 2026

3Q26 review: solid opex control

➢ OPM expansion on strong scale leverage despite GM contraction  
➢ Demand outlook remains promising even after US mid-term elections 
➢ Reiterating Buy (1) rating; trimming 12M TP to TWD320

Company profile 
Established in 1974, Hon Hai Precision Industry (Hon Hai) started out making plastic products, 
connector products and cable assemblies. It is now the largest electronics manufacturing service (EMS) 
provider globally in terms of design, manufacturing, global logistics and after-market service.

### Page 5

5

Hon Hai Precision Industry (2317 TT): 12 November 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 12 Nov 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 2
Hon Hai has incorporated 4 independent members into its 7-member board of directors in pursuit
of better governance. However, the company's Chairman also serves concurrently as President,
implying an absence of separation between board leadership and executive management.
Capital management 1 Hon Hai has declared >40% payout ratio for most of the years since 2014, and >50% in the past 5 years, pointing to a shareholder-friendly dividend policy.
Related party &
transaction 2
Revenue from related parties accounted for 1.6% of total revenue for 2025, while purchases
from related parties constituted 0.1% of cost of sales. We believe there is limited risk from
related-party transactions.
S Supply chain management 2 Hon Hai adheres to green design concepts and incorporates environmental factors and pollution prevention measures during the design and development stages. The company's green product design guidelines include 4 axes: "energy-saving, non-harmful, reduction and easily recyclable."
S Labor practices 2
Hon Hai recognises employees as the most important asset and continues to invest in its
workforce. In terms of safety, Hon Hai has upgraded its occupational safety and health
management system since 2019, and obtained the ISO45001 and CNS45001 certificates. The
company held online activities related to factory safety and health. Overall, we note that Hon Hai
has improved in terms of labour management in recent years. Hon Hai established a team to
implement data security management. Moreover, it plans to conduct data security training and
education regularly for all employees. Hon Hai aims to achieve zero data security incident in the
future.
S Product design & lifecycle management 2 Hon Hai adheres to green design concepts and incorporates environmental factors and pollution prevention measures during the design and development stages. The company's green product design guidelines include 4 axes: "energy-saving, non-harmful, reduction and easily recyclable."
Company specific Management Analyst comments
E GHG emissions 2
In November 2020, Hon Hai announced its commitment to achieving net zero greenhouse gas
emissions by 2050. Its Scope 1 and 2 GHG emissions decreased by 53.6% in 2025 compared
with 2020, exceeding its reduction target of 21%. That said, the company's actual energy-saving
rate in mainland China was 5.2% in 2025, successfully achieving its energy-saving goal of 4.2%.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

기아 (000270)

Company Brief 
자동차/모빌리티 
Analyst 김준성 
joonsung.kim@meritz.co.kr 
RA 배현서 
hyeonseo.bae@meritz.co.kr 배당은 지켜질 것  
3Q26 지배주주 순이익, 컨센서스 -38% 하회 
매출 31.6조원 (+10% YoY)으로 컨센서스에 부합했다. 출고 판매 (+10% YoY), 소
매 판매 (+8% YoY) 모두 높은 성장을 기록하며, 외형 확대에 기여했다. 
영업이익 1.82조원 (+24% YoY)으로 컨센서스를 -25% 하회했다. 전년 동기 대미 
관세 25% 부과에 따른 기저효과 +5,330억원 (2025년 11월부터 15%로 하향)이 
존재했고 기말 환율 (원/달러 -12% QoQ 강세) 변동에 따른 판매보증 충당부채 환
입 +6,700억원을 인식했음에도 불구하고, 신규 출시 저가 BEV 모델들의 판매 확대
에 따른 전사 평균 판매가격 하락  할인 경쟁 심화  원재료 및 운송비 상승으로 부
진한 실적을 기록했다. 
지배주주 순이익 1.29조원 (-10% YoY)으로 컨센서스를 -38% 하회했다. 영업 실적 
부진과 함께 원화 강세에 의한 외화자산 평가손실 및 관계사 실적 악화에 따른 지분
법 이익 감소 (-36% YoY)가 더해진 결과다.

6,000원 이상 배당 유지 전망 
4Q26 이후 실적 방향도 먹구름이 가득하다. 원/달러 환율의 YoY 강세 반영이 시작
된다. 미국 시장 내 경쟁력 확보를 위해 가장 중요한 원/엔 환율의 방향성 또한 비우
호적 흐름이 지속 중이다. 내수 시장을 포함해 전세계 주요 시장 내 중국산 차량과의 
경쟁 강도도 높아지고 있다. 원유 가격 뿐만 아니라 알루미늄, 구리 등 핵심 원재료 
가격의 상승 반영도 이어지고 있다. 
2026년 연간 지배주주 순이익 6.9조원 (-9% YoY)을 전망한다. 주주환원 총액은 축
소될 것이다. 다만 자사주 매입을 줄이더라도 배당은 전년 수준을 지킬 것으로 기대
한다. 참고로 2025년 실적에 기반한 주주환원은 자사주 매입 0.9조원, 배당 2.6조원 
(주당 6,800원)이었으며, 주주환원율은 48%였다. 현대차그룹은 Boston Dynamics
에 대한 증자를 이어가고 있다. 정의선 회장도 마찬가지다. 그리고 기아의 배당은 현
대차  현대글로비스의 배당과 함께 증자 재원으로 활용되고 있다. 6,000원 이상의 
배당 유지를 기대하며, 6% 이상 시가 배당 수익률 제시를 통해 주가 하방 경직성 확
보가 가능하다고 판단한다. 기업가치 상승 재개를 위해서는 기존 차량 제조  판매 사
업을 넘어 피지컬 데이터 디바이스 제공 사업 진출 가시성 확보가 필요하다. 
 Meritz Research  2026. 11. 2

Buy (20거래일 평균종가 대비 상승 여력 기준) 
적정주가 (12개월) 150,000원 
현재주가 (10.30) 95,600원 
상승여력 56.9%

2026.11.04   
LG에너지솔루션 
(373220)

ESS 수주 모멘텀 이어진다 Company Brief

3Q26 실적, 원-달러 환율 하락에도 시장 기대치 대폭 상회 
동사의 3Q26 실적은 매출 9.6조원(+59% YoY, +28% QoQ), 영업이익 7,560억원 
(+26% YoY, +568% QoQ)으로 원/달러 환율 하락에도 불구하고 시장기대치(매출 
8.8조원, 영업이익 3,217억원)를 크게 상회했다. 당사는 Stellantis와의 캐나다 
합작관계 종료에 따른 보상금 등을 포함해 약 4,250억원의 일회성 이익이 반영된 
것으로 추정한다. 이를 제외한 영업이익은 약 3,310억원으로, 시장기대치에 대체로 
부합한 것으로 판단된다. AMPC는 미국 ESS 생산량 증가와 GM 얼티엄셀즈 재가동에 
힘입어 전분기 대비 약 73% 증가한 4,169억원을 기록했다. 사업부문별로는 ESS의 
실적 개선이 두드러졌다. 상반기 배터리 패키징 병목으로 이연됐던 물량 출하가 
집중되면서 ESS 출하량은 전분기 대비 60% 가량 증가한 것으로 추정된다. 자동차 
전지도 유럽향 물량 회복과 GM 얼티엄셀즈 재가동으로 매출이 전분기 대비 약 
33%로 증가했으며, 고정비 부담 완화에 따라 적자폭도 뚜렷하게 축소된 것으로 
판단된다. 소형전지는 주요 고객사인 Tesla의 판매 호조에 따른 출하 증가로 매출 
회복과 수익성을 뒷받침한 것으로 보인다. 
4Q26 ESS 중심의 수익성 개선과 수주 모멘텀 지속될 전망 
미국 ESS 시장은 신재생 에너지 확대와 AI 데이터센터의 전력 수요 증가에 힘입어 
빠르게 성장하고 있다. 특히 정책 환경도 비PFE 공급망을 확보한 업체에 유리하게 
작용하고 있다. 동사는 3Q26 실적 컨퍼런스 콜에서 ESS 수주잔고가 약 
160GWh이며, 2028년까지 캐파의 대부분에 대응하는 물량을 확보했다고 밝혔다. 
또한 현재 다수의 고객사들과 2028년 이후 공급 물량을 협의하고 있어 연말까지 
추가적인 수주 모멘텀이 이어질 것으로 예상된다. 이를 바탕으로 4Q26에도 동사의 
본업 실적은 ESS 중심의 개선세를 이어갈 전망이다. 전분기에 반영된 대규모 일회성 
이익의 기저효과로 영업이익은 감소하겠지만, 이를 제외하면 출하량 증가와 가동률 
상승에 따른 점진적인 수익성 개선이 예상된다. 수익성 중심의 사업 효율화를 위해 
소형 IT용 파우치 사업을 축소하고 있다는 점도 중장기 손익 개선에 긍정적이다. 이를 
반영한 4Q26 매출과 영업이익은 각각 약 10.5조원(+63% YoY, +9% QoQ), 
4,150억원(흑자전환 YoY, -45% QoQ)으로 추정된다. 
매수 투자의견과 목표주가 유지 
동사에 대한 매수 투자의견과 목표주가를 유지한다. 북미 ESS 수요 증가와 공급망 
재편에 따른 신규 수주 확대가 중장기 실적 성장을 뒷받침할 전망이다. 특히 ESS용 
생산라인 전환과 가동률 상승이 본격화되면 매출 증가에 더해 고정비 부담 완화와 
AMPC 수혜 확대가 맞물리면서 수익성도 개선될 것으로 예상된다. ESS의 역할이 
신재생 에너지 연계용 저장장치를 넘어 AI 데이터센터의 안정적인 전력 공급을 
지원하는 핵심 인프라로 확대되고 있다는 점에도 주목할 필요가 있다. 이에 따라 
동사의 밸류에이션 역시 기존 EV 중심의 평가에서 벗어나 전력 인프라 산업의 
성장성을 반영하는 방향으로 재평가되어야 한다. 업종 내 실적 개선 가시성과 정책 
수혜가 뚜렷하고, 4Q26 신규 수주에 따른 주가 상승 가능성도 높다는 점에서 동사에 
대한 비중 확대 전략이 유효하다는 판단이다. 
Buy (Maintain)

 금융투자분석사와 그 배우자는 해당 기업의 주식을 보유하고 있지 않습니다.  
 당 보고서는 기관투자가 및 제 3자에게 E-mail등을 통하여 사전에 배포된 사실이 없습니다.  
 회사는 6개월간 해당 기업의 유가증권 발행과 관련 주관사로 참여하지 않았습니다. 
 당 보고서에 게재된 내용들은 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다. 
본 분석자료는 투자자의 증권투자를 돕기 위한 참고자료이며, 따라서, 본 자료에 의한 투자자의 투자결과에 대해 어떠한 목적의 증빙자료로도 사용될 수 없으며, 어떠한 경우에도 작성자 및 
당사의 허가 없이 전재, 복사 또는 대여될 수 없습니다. 무단전재 등으로 인한 분쟁발생시 법적 책임이 있음을 주지하시기 바랍니다.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
As expected, the RBA Monetary Policy Board (MPB) lifted
the cash rate 25bps to 4.85% at its November meeting.
This follows the increase at the previous (late-September)
meeting and is a marked turnaround from the outlook back
in August. The post-meeting media statement cited higher

global energy prices resulting from the Middle East conflict,
tighter domestic capacity pressures and an increase in
inflation expectations for the decision.
However, the decision was a split vote, 6 votes to 3, a
possibility we have flagged for some time. In the post-meeting
media conference, Governor Bullock explained that the
dissenting minority put more weight on the downside risks and
did not see signs of wages growth picking up as much as the

staff forecasts implied. Those members were reportedly more
confident that slower growth would prevent higher inflation
expectations becoming entrenched.
Financial conditions are restrictive and this is expected to
bring inflation down over time. Because the RBA assesses the
risks to the inflation outlook to still be skewed to the upside,
the outlook for coming meetings will still be for a decision
between holding and hiking. But we note that the post-meeting

statement omitted September’s language “including increasing
the cash rate target further if needed” from the commitment to
do what is necessary to get inflation down.
In the post-meeting media conference, the Governor
commented that after five hikes, this is probably a good time
to “take stock”. Together with the dissents, we read this as
confirming our current view that the MPB will keep rates
on hold in December. There is also considerable data flow

between now and then, which could go either way, including
the Q3 national accounts.
Similarly, a lot could happen between now and February, but
we see it as the next most likely timing for a hike, should one
eventuate. We still see that as a risk rather than a base case.
Revised forecasts published in the accompanying Statement
on Monetary Policy (SMP) show underlying inflation lingering
around current rates for longer. This contrasts with the
previous SMP forecasts in August, which showed trimmed

mean inflation returning to the 2–3%yr target range by late
2027 and a little below the 2.5%yr target midpoint in 2028.
We note, however, that the conditioning assumptions on oil
prices, which are based on futures prices, were at a local peak
at the time the forecasts were finalised.
RBA hikes cash rate to new high of 4.85%, a December pause likely
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
The RBA’s GDP growth forecasts for 2027 and 2028 were
both revised down –0.3%pts to 1.3% for 2027 and 1.5% in

2028, despite an upward revision to the near term. This is
well below even the RBA’s downbeat view of potential output
growth. Within the GDP aggregate, recent misses and leading
indicators have required upward revisions to the near-term
outlooks for business and dwelling investment, and downside
revisions to public demand. These revisions were offset further
out with downward revisions to the 2028 outlook for business
and dwelling investment and an upward revision to public

demand. Household consumption forecasts were revised down
across most of the profile.
With the unemployment rate already at 4.7% in August and
September – a rate the RBA did not forecast until end-2027 in
its August SMP forecasts – the RBA has lifted its unemployment
forecast to show a peak of 5.1% in at end-2028, the end of the
forecast horizon. This largely reflects stronger labour supply,
with forecasts for employment growth essentially unchanged.

We have repeatedly flagged that cost-of-living pressures are
likely to keep people in the labour force and thus boost the
participation rate. The RBA had previously not factored this
adequately into its forecasts but acknowledged in the SMP
that this mechanism had kept participation higher than it had
expected at the time of the August SMP.
However, because inflation surprised to the upside recently,
this additional labour supply has had no bearing on the

RBA’s assessment of capacity pressures. The RBA assesses
the domestic economy to still be a little tight, and tighter
than previously judged. The RBA’s framework for assessing
the degree of spare capacity or tightness in the economy
necessarily translates an upside surprise on inflation (or unit
labour costs) into a conclusion that the economy must be
tighter than previously judged. Indeed, the RBA’s estimate
of the NAIRU (unemployment rate consistent with inflation

stable at target) has been revised up to 4.75%.
The RBA highlighted that elevated inflation expectations may
be contributing to higher realised inflation. Consumer inflation
expectations have started to lift again but remain below where
they peaked earlier this year. However, the RBA appears
“RBA lifts cash rate 25bps to 4.85% in split
vote, citing “broadly based” domestic inflation
pressures, Middle East conflict and AI boom."

to be putting more attention on the inflation expectations
of firms and the frequency of mentions of pricing in their
liaison program. This is putting a lot of weight on proprietary
information that is not clearly validated by other, longer-
running data sources.

The AI boom is heavily cited in the SMP and other post-
meeting communication. The RBA has lifted its forecasts
for major trading partners, especially high-income Asian
economies that are the centre of high-tech manufacturing.
Demand for AI-related goods is also cited as a driver of both
global and domestic inflation. And although the RBA has
revised up its near-term forecasts for business investment
on the back of strong data centre activity, it is working on

the assumption that Australia does not become ‘a significant
regional hub for data centres’. Nor does it allow for any boost
to productivity trends from the technology, even in 2028. This
is a very different view of the risks from AI than the RBNZ
expressed last week. For the RBA, it is all about inflation, a
little bit about pressure on domestic construction resources,
and nothing about either an ugly unwind of the current
financing boom or a future productivity dividend.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: whiplash
total to $11.1bn. This is another clear marker of the scale of
Australia’s data-centre investment boom, which should feature
prominently in both business investment and imports in the Q3
National Accounts.
Offshore, the world has been focused on the US mid-term

elections. In the House, Senate and a series of State Governor
races, the Democrats experienced resounding victory. Come
2027, the Democrats will have a comfortable majority in both
houses of Congress and are expected to make use of this
strength to respond to voter concerns, particularly over cost-
of-living pressures. The election result also limits President
Trump’s ability to fund an extended military action against
Iran. Notably, both immediately before and after the election,

President Trump and the administration have exhibited a greater
interest in furthering negotiations with Iran. It is therefore not
a surprise that Brent oil has backed off its most recent highs
around USD118 last Friday, although the scale of the drop to
near USD93 yesterday is arguably too optimistic. Brent has since
traded back up to above USD99, still 16% below Friday’s peak.
While the initial reaction to the US election result was a
robust broad-based rally in major equity indexes, concerns

over the potential for tighter regulation of AI and the related
infrastructure buildout jolted US equities lower overnight – the
Nasdaq falling 5.5% and S&P500 4.4%, both a multiple of
the prior session’s gains. That said, US term interest rates and
FOMC rate expectations have also been scaled back; so, while
tech may come under greater scrutiny, a foundation for better
outcomes across the rest of the US economy may become
evident in time. US data out this week continued to suggest

their economy is in robust health overall. And, if tensions in
the Middle East abate, the FOMC’s concerns over US inflation
should subside. Gaining enough comfort for the FOMC to not
hike in December is a high bar, but the probability of further
hikes in 2027 should continue to fall.
Turning then to the UK, the Bank of England raised Bank Rate
by 25bps to 4.00% at their November meeting as expected.
On the Bank's baseline expectation, UK inflation is forecast to

rise from 3.5%yr at September to 5.00% by mid-2027. Activity
data has also surprised to the upside in recent months, though
"there remains limited evidence of knock-on effects on other
prices and wages".
In Australia, the RBA delivered another 25bp rate increase
at its November meeting, taking the cash rate to 4.85%. The
move was widely expected, reflecting the Board’s continued
concern around inflation following the recent run higher in
global energy prices, firmer domestic capacity pressures and

signs that inflation expectations have edged up. Inflation
is now not expected to return to the mid-point of the
target band until mid-2028. This is despite weaker growth
expectations and the unemployment rate trending up to
5.1% by end-2028 on the RBA’s forecasts.
Importantly, though, the decision was not unanimous, with
three members preferring to remain on hold given downside
risks to activity and the restraining influence of slower growth.
Governor Bullock’s suggestion that it was time to “take stock”

after five rate increases also struck a less hawkish note,
while the statement dropped September’s explicit reference
to raising rates further “if needed”. In our view, February
represents the next plausible opportunity for another hike, but
we see this only as a risk.
Policy’s effect on housing is becoming increasingly visible.
Cotality home prices fell a further 1.2% in October to now
be 6.7% below March’s peak. The cumulative price decline
is approaching the scale of previous housing corrections but

appears to have regained momentum after September’s rate
increase. Dwelling approvals saw a 5.1% headline gain in
September, but this was driven by volatile unit approvals while
detached house approvals fell sharply, suggesting higher rates
and the established-market downturn are beginning to weigh
on the construction pipeline.
Official data on nominal household spending meanwhile
rose 0.6% in September and 2.1% for Q3; however, much of
the recent acceleration looks to be price-driven, particularly

for fuel. Real spending volumes expanded by a much more
modest (but still solid) 0.7% in Q3.
The Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel provides a granular look at
income, spending and saving flows. The latest update suggests
that, beneath the resilient headline story, households are
increasingly being squeezed, particularly those with low savings
buffers. With another rate increase now delivered, housing
wealth declining and sentiment weak, Q3 increasingly looks like

the high-water mark for growth in consumer spending.
Lastly for Australia, in September the goods trade surplus
widened sharply to $2.8bn, with non-monetary gold trade
yet again adding considerable noise month-to-month. The
most notable development was another exceptionally strong
result for data-processing equipment imports, taking the Q3
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
In the press conference, Governor Bailey recognised the
nascent improvement in energy price pressures over the
past 48 hours and the effectiveness of monetary policy but
also conveyed that "Unless progress towards resolving the
conflict is made, or there is other news which softens the risk

to inflationary pressures, a further increase in Bank Rate may
be warranted". Governor Bailey's tone and comments made in
the minutes by other Committee members did not suggest this
was a decision that needs to be made in December, but rather
a risk to be judged as additional information is received. This
points to further tightening being more likely in early 2027
than before, and only if conditions / risks warrant.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
No deals are done
This week’s developments underscore our message that
a rate hike at the RBNZ’s December meeting is far from a
done deal. New Zealand’s labour market remains softer
than expected, and that will be noted by those on the

policy committee who question the extent of second-round
inflation pressures. The rout in the US mid-term elections
has also taken significant heat out of oil prices for now,
although it’s unclear whether that situation will hold through
to next month. Our view on the endpoint for this OCR cycle
remains unchanged, but there’s a growing risk that the RBNZ
could slow the pace of tightening in the new year.
The September quarter labour market surveys pointed to

ongoing softness in the jobs market. The unemployment
rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing a four-year run
of increases, and reaching its highest level since 2015.
Employment was softer than expected, and wage growth
remains subdued despite increasing cost-of-living pressures.
The Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS) showed a surprise
0.1% fall in employment in the September quarter, after a
downwardly revised 0.3% increase in the June quarter. That

went against the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI) measure
of filled jobs, which had suggested some upward momentum
emerging in the jobs market (and notably, the HLFS measure
had outpaced the MEI for the previous nine straight quarters).
The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in
the labour force participation rate from 70.6% to 70.4%.
These co-movements essentially reversed the surprisingly
large increases that we saw in the June quarter, which we had

suggested at the time were more likely due to sampling error
in the survey rather than a genuine surge.
We generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate,
which is less vulnerable to sampling errors – and the message
here is one of softness. Our best interpretation is that, to the
extent that jobs are growing, it’s not fast enough to absorb the
growth in the population (which itself is a fairly low bar, as net
migration remains at a low ebb).

Even with the economy returning to growth over the last
year or so, hiring activity has remained weak. This may be
an ongoing legacy of employment trends in previous years.
Businesses have told us that they felt burned by the severe
skill shortages that developed over 2021-23, and looked to
hold on to workers to the extent they could during the 2024-
25 downturn. The flipside of this is that they may now find
themselves overstaffed even as activity picks up again.

This shows up most strikingly in labour force transition rates.
The rise in unemployment over the last few years has not
been driven by an increase in job losses, but by accumulation
of people who, once out of work, are finding it increasingly
difficult to get back in again. That also extends to young
people trying to enter the workforce for the first time – youth
unemployment of around 25% is the highest in the history of
the survey going back to 1986.

The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September
quarter, in line with our expectations overall but a bit weaker
in the details – private sector wages were up by 0.5%, while
there was a surprisingly large 0.7% increase in public sector
wages. There was a notable jump in the number of employers
citing cost-of-living adjustments as a reason for increasing pay
rates – although the size of those increases varied a great deal,
with many substantially less than the headline inflation rate.

Potential RBNZ implications
The labour market data was unequivocally weaker than
expected by either us or the RBNZ. While likely not a
gamechanger for the cycle overall, it will add ammunition to the
arguments of the doves on the Monetary Policy Committee that
a pause in interest rate rises will make sense soon – perhaps in
December or early next year. It’s likely the case that interest rate
markets were over-pricing the odds of a hike at the 9 December

MPS, so the subsequent pullback in pricing makes sense.
The message from the RBNZ in the October meeting was to
be careful about pricing in "done deals", and that the outlook
Michael Gordon
Senior Economist

7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
will be data-dependent. As we noted at the time, alongside
measures of inflation expectations, the key domestic data
to watch ahead of the RBNZ’s 9 December meeting will
be indicators of what is happening to excess capacity (the
output gap), as this will be a key determinant of the Bank’s

assessment of the outlook for inflation over the coming year.
The RBNZ looks at a broad suite of indicators of labour market
slack, although their research found that only four of them
seem to have predictive power: the unemployment rate,
the vacancy to unemployment ratio, the QSBO measure of
labour as the main limiting factor to growth, and the rate of
transition between jobs. The first three of these all pointed
to a softening in the September quarter. (The fourth one is

calculated from income tax data, so it’s not publicly available
and even the RBNZ receives it only with a lag.)
In the eyes of the dovish MPC members, the softness in
the labour market indicators will likely push in the direction
of having greater comfort with the medium-term inflation
outlook. The outcome of the election, with the possibility of a
return to a dual inflation/employment mandate, could increase
the prominence of these factors. This likely won’t be an explicit

factor in the RBNZ’s reasoning at its December review, but it
may be in the minds of individual MPC members.
That said, it’s important to remember that in the RBNZ’s
forecasting framework, the GDP figures are the main
determinant of excess capacity in the economy. The most
recent GDP data tells us that the output gap probably
narrowed a bit through the June quarter, mainly reflecting
historical revisions. In light of the latest labour market figures,

there may be some downside risk to the outlook for Q3 GDP –
but there are conflicting indicators there, so it’s not that clear.
At last month’s surprise on-hold OCR decision, Governor
Breman lamented that there was no protocol for the RBNZ
to be able to signal how it was reading the economic data –
in particular the softer than expected Q3 CPI print – ahead
of the policy meeting. In that light, it will be interesting to
see whether they use some already-scheduled speaking

engagements to signal what they make of the labour market
data. Top of mind is Chief Economist Conway's 10 November
speech to the UBS Australasia Conference (pointedly titled
“How is the NZ economy tracking”). Governor Breman is also
speaking to the Institute of Directors on 19 November.
Our view remains that the December OCR hike is more likely
than not. But there will be a clear debate to be had between
the MPC hawks and doves, and it may again come down to a

vote. We still expect a 4% peak in the OCR for this cycle, but
the time taken to get there could be more flexible. Takes along
the lines of "3% is the terminal rate" seem rather hasty on the
basis of a single data release.
Aside from how the Committee interprets the labour market
data, the key considerations in December will be: the RBNZ’s
surveys of inflation expectations by households and businesses;
whether the US mid-term elections lead to a sustained de-

escalation in the Middle East and a pullback in oil prices; and
whether developments in the AI space exacerbate some MPC
members’ concerns about the global economic environment.
And then of course there’s the result of the General Election,
with voting closing on Saturday evening. The actual formation
of a government is likely to take weeks of negotiation, so the
outcome may not be known by the 9 December MPS but at least
the range of possibilities should be clearer. We’ll publish our

initial thoughts on the election results early on Monday morning.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Oct Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Nov 12, Last: –0.4, Westpac f/c: 1.4
We’re forecasting a 1.4% rise in retail spending in October.
While that would be a solid increase, it’s in large part due to
the 11% rise in petrol prices over the past month. Stripping out

fuel spending, we’re forecasting a 0.6% rise in core spending
categories. Core spending has been very choppy recently,
and the increase we’re assuming follows falls in the past few
months. A result in line with our expectations would leave the
trend in core spending relatively flat since the start of this year
with the rise in fuel prices weighing on spending in other areas.
AUS: Nov Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment (index)
Nov 10, Last: 84.4
Consumer sentiment fell 4.7% to 80.4 in Oct, returning to the

deeply pessimistic levels seen earlier in the year. Responses
over the course of the survey week showed a very sharp
deterioration after the RBA rate hike.
The Nov survey is in the field over the week ended Nov 7.
Interest rates will again factor with the RBA raising the cash
rate by another 25bps to 4.85%, an 18yr high. Average pump
prices also pushed higher, the national average just under
$2.40/litre, while the housing market downturn has continued

to broaden and deepen. The US mid-term election results may
have some effects on sentiment locally as well.
AUS: Q3 Housing Finance Approvals (%qtr)
Nov 11 Last: –5.2, Westpac f/c: –10
Market f/c: –8.5, Range: –15 to –5
The total value of housing finance approvals fell The value of
new dwelling finance approvals excl. refinance fell 5.2%qtr,
the largest quarterly drop in three years. Annual growth eased
to 7.3%yr. The weakness was accounted for mainly by lower

lending volumes: loan numbers were down 4.0%qtr (–0.6%yr),
while average loan size also contributed negatively.
Q3 is expected to see an even bigger decline with the full
impact of interest rate rises and tax policy changes showing
through. The value of both loan enquiries and turnover fell by
around 10% in the quarter. We expect total finance approvals
to show a 10% drop as well, with investor loan approvals likely
to be down 16%qtr and owner occupier approvals down 6%qtr.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
US: Oct CPI (%mth)
Nov 11, Headline, Last: 0.6, Market f/c: 0.3, Westpac f/c: 0.4
US consumer prices rose by 0.6% in September, although
half of the gain was due to energy inflation, with core prices
instead up 0.3%. Over the year, prices were reported to have

risen 3.7%yr overall and 2.6%yr excluding food and energy.
Constructively, ex food, energy and shelter, annual inflation
met the FOMC’s medium-term target of 2.0%yr.
Come October, energy price pressures are set to recede
given the pre-election run higher in oil prices came late in the
month. A softer print for shelter is also likely to moderate core
momentum. We see a 0.4% gain for the headline measure and
a 0.2% result for core inflation as most probable. Looking to

2027, disinflation is expected to continue at a measured pace
towards the medium-term target.

Key points
• The Cotality home value index – covering
all of Australia – fell 1.2% in October
following a 1.3% decline in September
and 1.4% falls in August and July.

Annual growth has turned negative,
running at –2.8%yr with prices down
6.7% from their peak in March.
• Across the major cities, Brisbane and
Perth led the declines in the October
month, recording falls of 2.0% and
1.7% respectively, but the correction
remains more pronounced in Sydney
(prices down a further 1.6%mth to be
off 9.4%yr) with Melbourne also off
more sharply over the year (–7.6%yr)
but recording a milder October
(–0.8%mth). Adelaide remains relatively

resilient, recording a 1.2% decline in
the month but prices still up 3.7%yr.

number of sales, in rolling 3mth
terms, down 5.6%qtr and 22.5%yr.

forecasts are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially
from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The Cotality home value index, covering all of Australia, fell
1.2% in October with slight revisions to previous months
again shaving a further 0.3% off prices. The correction
has now seen prices decline 6.7% from their March peak

with annual growth firmly in negative at –2.8%yr. Turnover
continues fall as well, down 22.5%yr. Housing-related
tax policy changes, interest rate rises and heightened
uncertainty have triggered a sharp contraction in markets.
The downturn is now comparable to the previous two
housing market corrections. Nationally, the downturn in
2022-23 saw prices decline 7.5% and turnover fall 29% peak
to trough. The 2017-18 correction saw prices fall 8.6% and

turnover by 24%, with larger falls in Sydney and Melbourne.
Note that unless specified, figures in titles, tables and
commentary are in non‑seasonally adjusted terms, consistent
with the ‘headline’ figures reported by Cotality. However,
figures shown in charts are in seasonally adjusted terms.
Preliminary estimates of turnover show rolling 3mth volumes
down 5.6%qtr in October, previous estimates again revised
lower. The monthly pattern suggests declines re‑accelerated

through September‑October after stabilising somewhat in July‑
August. The RBA's interest rate rise in late September and a
further expected tightening likely contributed to the shift.
The detail shows Brisbane and Perth are now leading the
way in terms of monthly price declines although corrections
to date remain significantly larger in Sydney and Melbourne
while smaller capitals and regional areas continue to see much
milder downturns. Across price tiers, all segments are now

showing similar rates of decline after top price tiers had seen
a more pronounced and earlier downturn. 'Top tier' segments
have tended to lead recent price cycles.
Looking across jurisdictions,
• Sydney prices dropped 1.6% in October having averaged
similar‑sized declines over the previous five months. While
all segments and tiers continue to record falls, the last few
months has seen the pace of decline moderate a touch in the
top tier segments that have led cycles in the past, although

they are still a long way from stabilisation.
• Melbourne prices declined 0.8%mth, in line with
September's move and continuing the slower pace of
retreat. As noted, this likely reflects the weaker starting
point coming into the correction, prices already at 5½ year
lows. Interestingly, the detail shows the moderation in price
declines in recent months has been more pronounced for
'top tier' units where prices are back where they were in
2016.
Housing downturn deepens

### Page 3

forecasts are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially
from these forecasts.
3WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
• Brisbane prices are tracking a very fast rate of decline
that is also seeing large downward revisions. Prices fall
2% in October with September and August now both
showing 2.1% falls, revised down from 1.5% and 1.6%

respectively (August having previously been estimated at
just 1%, July's move having seen a similar series of revisions
–1.2% to –1.8%). The combined effect has taken the rolling
3mth change to –6.1%qtr from –4.7%qtr last months and
–2.7%qtr in August and –0.6%qtr in July. At 0.4%yr, annual
price growth has virtually stalled flat having run at 18%yr as
recently as May. All tiers and segments are recording similar
rates of decline.
• Perth is seeing something very similar, prices dropping a

further 1.7% in October, with September's decline revised
down to 2.1% from –1.2% and August's to 2.0% from –1.7%.
At 3.9%yr, annual growth is still positive but off steeply
from the 24% pace back in May. As with Brisbane, all tiers
and segments are recording similar rates of decline.
• Adelaide dwelling prices posted a somewhat milder 1.2%
decline in October, , September's –1.3% decline marked
down to –1.7%. Annual price growth slowed to 3.7%yr,

similar to Perth's but with less of a whiplash from strong
gains to large declines. Turnover has also been much
steadier, sales holding flat over the last three months and
down just 12.4%yr.
• The smaller capitals were again more mixed, price growth
still positive in Darwin (0.6%mth, 9.7%yr), relatively resilient
in Hobart (+0.5%mth, 5.5%yr) but weaker in Canberra
(–0.7%mth, –3.7%yr).
• Regional markets showed a 0.9% decline in Qld but slightly
milder declines in NSW (–0.7%) and Victoria (0.6%). Annual

prices growth is in the 1.4‑3.5%yr range.
All up, the October updates make for bleak reading. With the
RBA widely expected to raise interest rates again, the near
term outlook remains very challenging. Next week's consumer
sentiment update will show how this is shaping housing‑
related sentiment – so far, consumer price expectations have
weakened but stopped short of swinging to the clear negative
consensus that emerged in the 2022‑23 correction. That now

looks to be at risk. Either way, momentum looks set to remain
weak into year‑end and, barring a significantly improved
inflation and interest rate outlook, well into 2027.

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: the source and persistence of inflation
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
11:16 October 30 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia this week, all eyes were on the September CPI. Headline inflation rose 0.8% (4.3%yr) in
the month, largely owing to a 9.4% jump in fuel prices and a seasonal surge in domestic and
international holiday travel & accommodation. The underlying inflation pulse also proved hotter than

anticipated for Q3 overall, up 1.0% (3.7%yr).

### Page 2

While some parts of the basket are seeing disinflation – housing in particular looks to be cooling faster
than expected as builders lure sales with discounts – other components are still showing persistence.
Highlighting this is market services ex volatiles, which the RBA watches closely as an indicator of price
pressures related to domestic capacity. In Q3, this metric rose 1.2% (3.2%yr), and the monthly detail
points to momentum holding up – in line with our expectations for another chunky read in Q4.

With inflation running well above the RBA’s August forecasts, and some of the energy risks flagged
then materialising, we continue to expect the Monetary Policy Board will deliver a 25bp rate hike to
4.85% in November. Beyond November, another rate hike in February is a risk, but is not currently part
of our base case.
Across the Tasman, the RBNZ this week surprised market expectations for a 25bp rate hike, deciding
instead to wait for further information before taking the next step with policy. The decision was made

4:3, with the Governor in the ‘on hold’ camp. A 25bp hike in December to 3.00% and further tightening
in 2027 (Westpac NZ forecasts a peak of 4.00% in September) seems probable; however, each
decision will depend on fuel prices, inflation expectations and evidence of second-round pricing
pressures, along with the evolution of global economic risks.
In the US, the FOMC also held rates steady at its October meeting. Based on prior comments by key
members, the decision was not a surprise, and neither were Governor Cook and Cleveland Fed

President Hammack’s dissents in favour of a 25bp hike. The Committee remains positive on economic
activity, characterising it in the statement as “expanding at a strong pace” (an upgrade from “solid” in
September). Chair Warsh also subsequently emphasised the breadth of economic growth outside of
tech-related investment (evinced by Q3 GDP the day after; see below).
The Committee refrained from spotlighting key price risks in the statement. However, inflation “remains

elevated” and the FOMC are determined to achieve a “timely” and sustainable return of annual inflation
to 2.0%yr after 6 years above target. This guidance and the “accumulation of more information will help
the Committee determine the policy stance” suggests another 25bp hike is likely in December.
Thereafter, the FOMC will remain attentive to any broadening in inflation and related risks through
2027; although, in the absence of another shock, we expect them to remain on hold.

The Q3 GDP report certainly supported Chair Warsh’s view on the breadth of growth, the 3.4%
annualised gain for GDP benefitting from a 3.8% annualised increase in household consumption,
12.7% growth in non-residential investment as well as a robust increase in government spending.
Notably though, the contribution from business investment was effectively offset by net exports’ drag
given most of the componentry related to AI infrastructure investment is imported. This is not to say that

US firms won’t benefit from the investment once complete, but simply that much of the initial return
goes to the foreign businesses who provide the necessary capital equipment.
On consumption, the monthly PCE data implies downside risks remain for the consumer, the increase
in nominal spending seen in the month requiring dissaving and, on a real basis, consumption flat in the
month following a 0.5% gain in August. Cost-of-living pressures and uncertainty over the outlook is

### Page 3

Related articles
expected to see household consumption growth average around trend over the coming year, albeit with
considerable volatility quarter to quarter. This should see PCE inflation slow back towards its 2.0%yr
medium-term target as the FOMC desires, from 3.6%yr and 3.1%yr for the headline and core
measures respectively as of September.
Across the Atlantic, the ECB also left its key policy rates unchanged in October. Of note from the
meeting communications, energy prices continue to pose upside risks for inflation, but also downside

risks for growth. "While the full effects of the energy shock are still unfolding, underlying inflation has
remained broadly stable so far and wages do not show a material response". "Financing conditions
have [also] tightened", "which is likely to weigh on inflation and growth". In the press conference,
President Lagarde noted that, between the October and December meetings, a significant amount of
new economic information will be released, allowing forecasts and risks assessments to be tested.

Based on the October meeting communications, we continue to expect a rate hike at the December
meeting but believe this will be prove to be the last hike of the cycle. Note though, the spectrum of risks
Europe faces is broad and skewed towards inflation in the near term, hence the Council’s guidance will
remain hawkish. President Lagarde also announced today that she will step down as ECB President in
February ahead of the scheduled conclusion of her term next October.

The scenario, forecasts and nowcasts of the Economic Research

### Page 2

UNITED STATES 
The US economy is expected to grow above its potential in 2026, with an average annual growth rate of 2.2%, close to the 2025 rate (2.1%). 
This apparent resilience to the uncertainty, tariff and energy shocks, together with above-trend productivity growth, masks K-shaped growth, 
driven by investment linked to AI-optimism and consumption by the wealthiest amid a wealth effect (historically high stock market valuations). 
Inflation overshooting is set to continue (3.5% in 2026) at least through 2028, largely because of the rise in oil prices and tariffs – although the 
impact of these latter appears to be less significant than expected. The labour market is showing clear signs of improvement that should bring 
the unemployment rate down toward 4.0%. Given this shift in price and employment risks amid dynamic growth, the FOMC proceeded with a 
first rate hike at September’s meeting and we expect two further 25bp increases in December 2026 and January 2027, bringing the Fed Funds 
target range at 4.25% - 4.50%.

CHINA 
Economic growth rebounded to 4.5% y/y in Q3 2026 (1.2% q/q) after 4.3% y/y in Q2 and 5.0% in Q1. It is projected to reach 4.6% in 2026 as a 
whole, down from 5% in 2025. Growth remains characterised by a K-shaped trajectory, and the gap between the strong performance of exports 
and the fragility of sectors that rely on domestic demand continues to widen. Retail sales barely increased in the first ten months of 2026, the 
crisis in the property sector continues and household confidence remains low. Yet new fiscal policy measures could help support investment 
and private consumption in the very short term. Deflationary pressures are persisting, given weakness in domestic demand. Energy prices and 
electronic goods prices are increasing, producer price inflation is accelerating (5.7% y/y in October), but consumer price inflation remains low 
(0.7% y/y).

EUROZONE 
The economy has shown resilience in the face of the energy shock: GDP grew by 0.4% q/q in Q3, and the 1.0% growth carry-over for 2026 
supports our forecast of 1.1% for the year as a whole (compared to 1.3% in 2025) with the risk tilted to the upside. Growth is expected to 
strengthen to 1.6% in 2027, bolstered by increased spending on defence and infrastructure, as well as the global investment cycle in AI. Annual 
average inflation is thus expected to reach 3.1% in 2026 (from 2.1% in 2025), and 3.2% in 2027. Given persistent inflation and resilient growth, 
we anticipate another 25bp hike from the ECB in December 2026, bringing the deposit rate to 2.75%, followed by an extended pause. That 
said, stronger than expected second-round effects could lead to more tightening than our current baseline.

### Page 3

FRANCE 
GDP contracted by 0.1% q/q in Q3 2026 (following stagnation in Q2), weighed down by a decline in aeronautics production (delay in the 
delivery of inputs) but supported by service sector output and purchases of electric vehicles. Overall, growth is expected to slow to 0.3% in 
2026 (after 0.9% in 2025) before rebounding to 0.8% in 2027. In parallel, inflation should reach 2.5% in 2026 (compared with 0.9% in 2025), 
before edging down slightly in 2027 (2.4%).

UNITED KINGDOM 
UK GDP growth would accelerate to 1.6% in 2026 (1.2% in 2025). The economy has so far weathered both the energy price shock and domestic 
political volatility, boosted by domestic demand, notably investment. After a series of strong prints from Q1 to Q3, real GDP growth is expected 
to decelerate in the last quarter of the year, as higher energy prices should feed inflation. It should then slow to 1.3% in 2027 on the back of 
higher inflation and more restrictive monetary conditions. Inflation should climb to 3.9% in Q4 2026, peaking in Q1 2027 (at 4.5%) before 
gradually slowing. Still, it is set to remain stubbornly above target, leading to mounting second-round effects risks. In response, the BoE has 
finally raised its Bank Rate to 4.00% early November. If a second-rate hike looks likely in February, this is not, for now, our call and our baseline 
scenario projects that diminishing inflation will allow monetary easing to resume in the second half of 2027, with two cuts taking the Bank 
Rate down to 3.50% by year-end.

JAPAN 
Following 1.2% in 2025, Japan's GDP growth is expected to decelerate to 0.9% in 2026, before rebounding to 1.0% in 2027. Higher inflation 
and production costs associated with the energy shock are expected to weigh on the economy’s overall performance. On the other hand, the 
latter is supported by fiscal policy and AI-related investment. Inflation has generally overshot the 2% y/y target since 2022. Accordingly, the 
Bank of Japan initiated a cautious process of “adjustment in the degree of monetary accommodation” in 2024, lifting the policy rate to 1.25% 
so far (previously negative) – the highest since 1995. We expect two more hikes by the end of Q1 2027, followed by a return to a more cautious 
pace until the terminal rate of +2.5% is reached in H2 2028. Japan is facing long-term rates pressure, illustrated by historically high 10- and  
30-year yields, probably fueled by the level of public debt and the pace of monetary adjustment.

EXCHANGE RATES 
We expect Fed hikes to temporarily offset structural headwinds and support the dollar. EUR/USD would target 1.14 by end-2026, before the 
dollar's structural downtrend resumes toward 1.20 by end-2027, although political and fiscal risk may weigh on the euro next year. The USD/JPY 
appreciation would continue but at a more moderate pace, with the BoJ still behind the curve despite its hikes. By contrast, GBP/USD would 
see relative support underpinned by a modest fiscal risk premium under the new Labour government, and resilient growth.

Document 451: What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22)

WHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM
THE MEASURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO
TURN THE SCREW?
Marianne Mueller and Pascal Devaux
issue 19
21 October 2026
The 2022 energy crisis clearly highlighted the issue of electricity prices in Europe — and, more broadly, the issue of energy prices. As
a result, European manufacturers’ competitiveness has been severely undermined when compared with the United States and China.

By reigniting tensions over electricity prices, the conflict that began in the Middle East in late February 2026 further underscores the
continent’s vulnerability to fossil fuel imports, despite the expansion of renewable energy. Faced with an environment of potential sharp
and sudden price rises, European governments do not have the same room for manoeuvre.
While the additional costs borne by European manufacturers are mainly linked to the share of gas in national electricity mixes, we will

first illustrate that, in Germany, grid operation and maintenance costs, as well as taxation, are also contributing significantly to these
costs.
We will then explore the solutions proposed by the German government under the new European measures regulating state aid to
electricity-intensive industries.
Finally, our estimates will show that, while these measures make Germany more competitive against some European countries, they
do not place it on an equal competitive footing with the United States or China. Nevertheless, Germany has made significant progress

in decarbonising and making its electricity mix more flexible from a European perspective. This should help to ensure that electricity costs
are reduced in the medium term.

### Page 2

2
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoInsight 19 / 21 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
worldWHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM THE MEA- SURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO TURN THE SCREW?
GAS: A KEY FACTOR IN THE ADDITIONAL ELECTRICITY COSTS
FACED BY EUROPEAN INDUSTRY
European manufacturers are still paying significantly higher elec -
tricity costs than their American and Chinese competitors. In 2019,

there was a 30% difference between the prices charged to European
electricity-intensive industries (corresponding to the lower end of the
industrial price range) and the average US industrial price. In 2025,
it stood at nearly 100% according to the IEA (2026) 1, after peaking at
an average of 148% between 2022 and 2023. There is currently a 50%
difference with their Chinese competitors2.
The disruption to Russian gas supplies since 2022 is behind the rising

gas prices, particularly as the EU has had to turn to liquefied natural
gas3 (LNG), which is more expensive than gas transported via pipe -
line and, above all, exposes Europe to global gas market conditions.
The growth of LNG has, in fact, intensified competition between the
major consumption regions, primarily Europe and Asia. European and
Asian prices now tend to move more in tandem (Chart 1). However, in
China, the prevalence of coal — a widely available local resource —

and a more regulated electricity price help to keep industrial prices
lower. At the same time, the United States enjoys plentiful domestic
gas, which is produced at a low cost, driven by the sharp rise in shale
oil and gas production over the past decade. By contrast, since 2022,
Europe has been more exposed to the cost of imported gas (90% of its
total consumption, according to Eurostat), while the United States and
China have enjoyed plentiful domestic resources.

Fluctuations and the level of gas prices are passed on to Eu -
ropean electricity prices via the ‘merit order’ marginal pricing
mechanism, with the wholesale price determined by the margi -
nal cost of the last (and most expensive) electricity source called
upon to meet demand.
As a result, gas plays a decisive role, with gas-fired power sta -
tions setting the price in around 40% of cases in Europe4, with si-
gnificant variations depending on national electricity mixes. It is

close to this average in Germany, below it in Spain (26%) and
France (17%), and above it in Italy (73%) 5 in 2025 6 7. Therefore,
electricity prices are still sensitive to fluctuations in gas prices,
even when electricity is not generated from gas (Chart 2). This
pass-through is particularly pronounced when demand rises
and requires the use of more expensive sources, or when supply
constraints arise (for example, during extreme heatwaves that
reduce wind-power generation or disrupt the cooling of nuclear

power plants).
1 Prices – Electricity 2026 – Analysis – IEA
2 The difference may be even larger for other manufacturers who do not receive the same support as electricity-intensive sectors.
3 Imports of Russian gas (via pipeline and LNG) accounted for around 45% of total European imports in 2021, compared with 14% in Q2 2026 (Bruegel, natural gas imports).
4 ACER, 2026, Key developments in EU electricity and gas markets, 2026 Monitoring report.
5 In Italy, in response to sharply rising gas prices in 2026, the government is currently negotiating a potential option of capping the prices of gas supplied to power stations in

order to limit the rise in the wholesale price of electricity. However, this can only be a temporary general mechanism, with the potentially major cost being borne by public funds
and/or households.
6 Eurelectric, 2026, Power Barometer.
7 EMBER, 2026, Latest energy shock reminds Europe of its risky reliance on gas.
8 Energy shock: 2026 vs. 2022 dashboard – 2nd October 2026 – Economic Studies – BNP Paribas
However, for the time being, the 2026 energy crisis is having a far

smaller effect on electricity prices than in 2022. On the one hand, the
2026 peak reached by the European TTF benchmark is still well below
that recorded in 20228.
In addition, there has been significant progress in decarbonising Eu -
ropean electricity mixes since 2022, which has helped to mitigate, on
average, the pass-through of gas prices to electricity prices.

SOME EUROPEAN COUNTRIES HAVE MANAGED TO STAY
COMPETITIVE, BUT GERMANY IS STILL THE EXCEPTION
Electricity prices partly vary due to countries’ electricity mixes
(Chart 3). Electricity-intensive manufacturers in countries where the
share of fossil energy is high face higher costs (Poland, Italy and Ire -
land) (Chart 4). Conversely, countries with a less carbon-intensive mix

(particularly France and Spain in recent times) are less directly ex -
posed to these fluctuations, with costs closer to the United States and
China (Chart 5).
The situation in Germany is paradoxical, as, despite progressively
greening its electricity mix and wholesale electricity prices (averaging
90 EUR/MWh over the 2023–2025 period) and average electricity costs
(across all industrial sectors) in line with the European average, the most
energy-intensive industries face higher costs than the European average

(and even more so compared with international competitors). This is
mainly due to grid costs and a tax burden above EU standards (Chart 6).
9 Eurostat data are used to compare prices with the rest of the EU. However, given that electricity prices are not uniform for businesses at a European level, there may be differences
between Eurostat and national sources. Jäger P., 2025, “Cautionary tale or model for Europe? How member states should react to Germany’s new electricity subsidy”, Hertie School,

Jacques Delors Centre.
10 P. Devaux, 2026, “European Union: low carbon transition and energy sovereignty, a path fraught with obstacles”
In the first half of 2026, for the most electricity-intensive industries,
the difference compared with the European average was 9%, based on
the prices excluding taxes and levies provided by Eurostat. This gap is
mainly due to the cost of accessing the grid. If all taxes and levies are
included, the difference rises to 21% (Chart 7)9.

EUROPE’S APPROACH OF ADOPTING BOTH STRUCTURAL
PROGRAMMES AND TARGETED SUPPORT MEASURES
European programmes to support the low-carbon transition pursue
objectives relating to climate action (reducing greenhouse gas emis -
sions), sovereignty10 (increasing energy self-sufficiency) and competi -
tiveness (reducing the volatility and costs associated with fossil fuels).
The RePowerEU programme, which was launched in 2022 in response
to the energy crisis, primarily aims to reduce imports of Russian gas

and, more generally, to accelerate the low-carbon transition. Although
Europe has reduced its dependence on imports of Russian fossil fuels,
there has still not been enough progress around the energy transition
and cross-border connectivity. This is due to governance constraints
